\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Lambda\left(\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+k})},\left\|\partial_{t}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+k})},\left\|\rho_{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+k})},\left\|\partial_{t}\rho_{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+k})}\right)\omega[a_{f}].

This concludes the proof of the estimate (
6.15
) of the lemma, thanks to Sobolev embedding.
∎

We finally obtain the following result, yielding some control on the seminorms of the Penrose function in terms of the initial data only.

Corollary 6.13
.

There hold

(6.18)

\displaystyle\omega[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}),

(6.19)

\displaystyle\Xi[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}]_{\mathrm{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}),\ \ 2\mathrm{M}<m-11-d/2,

(6.20)

\displaystyle\Omega\left[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}).

Proof.

We combine the estimates (
6.9
)–(
6.10
)–(
6.11
) of Lemma
6.10
with (
6.15
)–(
6.16
)–(
6.17
) of Lemma
6.12
. We first get for
3+3d/2\leq\ell<m-3

\displaystyle\omega[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}]

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\left(\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+\ell})},\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\rho_{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+\ell})}\right)\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\|(1+t)\partial_{t}^{i}f\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}^{\ell}_{r})}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\left(\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+\ell})},\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}^{d+\ell}_{\sigma})}\right)\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}),

for
\sigma>d/2
. Hence, by using the equation on
\varrho
with Remarks
2.21
and Lemma (
2.22
), and by taking
\ell<m-3-d
, we can use (
6.13
) from Lemma
6.11
to obtain (
6.18
). Next, we have for
4+\mathrm{M}+d/2<\ell<m-6

\displaystyle\Xi[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}]]_{\mathrm{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\left(\underset{i=0,1,2,3,4}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{1+\mathrm{M}+\ell})},\underset{i=0,1,2,3,4}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\rho_{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{1+\mathrm{M}+\ell})}\right)\underset{i=0,1,2,3,4}{\sup}\,\|\partial_{t}^{i}f\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}^{\ell}_{r})}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\left(\underset{i=0,1,2,3,4}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{1+\mathrm{M}+\ell})},\underset{i=0,1,2,3,4}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}^{1+\mathrm{M}+\ell}_{\sigma})}\right)\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}),

for
\sigma>d/2
. Using (
6.14
) from Lemma
6.11
, the equation on
\varrho
, Remark
2.21
and Lemma (
2.22
) with
\ell+1+\mathrm{M}<m-6
, we deduce (
6.19
). Finally, we also have for
7+3d/2<\ell<m-3-d

\displaystyle\Omega[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}]

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\left(\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{d+\ell})},\underset{i=0,1}{\sup}\left\|\partial_{t}^{i}f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}^{d+\ell}_{\sigma})}\right)\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}),

for
\sigma>d/2
and as before, we obtain (
6.20
).
∎

6.5.
Elliptic estimates through pseudodifferential analysis

Let
T\in(0,T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})
. In view of the equation (
6.1
) on
h=\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varrho
obtained in Proposition
6.1
, we initiate the study of the equation

(6.21)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\right)\left[\widetilde{H}\right]=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\ \ 0\leq t\leq T,

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
is a given source term defined on
(0,T)
. Given a solution
\widetilde{H}
to this equation, we want to derive an
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})
estimate of
\widetilde{H}
in terms of
\widetilde{R}
. This will be possible thanks to the Penrose stability condition satisfied by
(f(t),\varrho(t))
(see the forthcoming Proposition
6.18
).

Note that the operator involved in the equation (
6.21
) depends on
\varrho
and
f
, which are defined for all times.

Following
[
HKR16
]
, we would like to link the operator
\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}
which appears in (
6.21
) to a pseudodifferential operator of order
0
in time-space.
For
\gamma>0
(which will be chosen large enough in the end, but always independent of
\varepsilon
), we set

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(t,x):=e^{\gamma t}H(t,x),\ \ \widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(t,x):=e^{\gamma t}\mathcal{R}(t,x).

We can rewrite (
6.21
) as

(6.22)

\displaystyle H(t,x)-\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\Big[e^{\gamma\bullet}\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H\Big](t,x)=\mathcal{R}(t,x),\ \ 0\leq t\leq T.

To study the solution
H
on
[0,T]
, we will extend the equation (
6.22
) on the whole line
\mathbb{R}
. This is possible thanks to the following lemma, which can be seen as a causality principle for the equation (
6.22
). We postpone its proof to the end of Section
6.5
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
T>0
. Assume that
\widetilde{H}_{1},\widetilde{H}_{2},\widetilde{S}_{1},\widetilde{S}_{2}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfy the equation (
6.21
) with

\quad\widetilde{H}_{i}|_{(-\infty;0]}=0,\quad i=1,2,

and

\widetilde{S}_{1}|_{[0,T]}=\widetilde{S}_{2}|_{[0,T]}.

Then

\widetilde{H}_{1}|_{[0,T]}=\widetilde{H}_{2}|_{[0,T]}.

We thus consider the same equation as (
6.22
) satisfied on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
by a solution
H
with source
\mathcal{R}
where

•

the source
\mathcal{R}(t,x)
is equal to the original source on
[0,T]
and is equal to zero for
t<0
and for
t>T
,

•

the solution
H(t,x)
is equal to zero for
t<0
.

In what follows, we use the following notation for the Fourier transform in time-space, and we also refer to Section
C
in the Appendix:

\displaystyle\forall(\tau,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d},\ \ \mathcal{F}_{t,x}g(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{-i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}g(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x.

For symbols of the form
a(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}
and in the Schwartz class, we rely on the quantification

\displaystyle\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a)(h)(t,x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}a(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)\mathcal{F}_{t,x}h(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}k.

The measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is the discrete measure. Note that the symbols we shall use are defined on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
in the physical space, thanks to the extension procedure from Section
6.3
. Note also that we shall handle symbols defined in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the
k
variable, even if we only use them for
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in the formula.

The following lemma now provides the link between the intego-differential operator of (
6.22
) and pseudodifferential operators.

Lemma 6.15
.

We have

\displaystyle e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\Big[e^{\gamma\bullet}H\Big](t,x)=p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a_{f})(H)(t,x),\ \ \text{on}\ \ \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
a_{f}
is defined in
6.8
.
In particular, the equation (
6.22
) on
H
reads

(6.23)

\displaystyle H-\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho)\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a_{f})(\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H)=\mathcal{R},\ \ \text{on}\ \ \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle H(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}\mathcal{F}_{t,x}H(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}k,

therefore

\displaystyle e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}(e^{\gamma\bullet}H)(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-\gamma(t-s)}e^{i(\tau s+k\cdot(x-(t-s)v)}ik\cdot\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)\mathcal{F}_{t,x}H(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}k,

because
H
is
0
on negative times. We use the Fubini theorem (which holds since
\gamma>0
) and get

e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}(e^{\gamma\bullet}h)(t,x)=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}\\
\left(\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)(t-s)}ik\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-ik\cdot v(t-s)}\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathcal{F}_{t,x}H(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}k,

therefore, setting
s^{\prime}=t-s

\displaystyle e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}(e^{\gamma\bullet}h)(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-ik\cdot vs}\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathcal{F}_{t,x}H(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}k

\displaystyle=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{i(\tau t+k\cdot x)}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(t,x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathcal{F}_{t,x}H(\tau,k)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}kw

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x,))\mathrm{Op}(a_{f})(h)(t,x).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Having in mind a semiclassical approach (see also Apppendix
C
), we introduce the following quantization.

Definition 6.16
.

For any symbol
b(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)
on
R\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}
, we set for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

\displaystyle b^{\varepsilon}(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)

\displaystyle:=b(t,x,\varepsilon\gamma,\varepsilon\tau,\varepsilon k),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(b)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(b^{\varepsilon}).

Lemma 6.17
.

We have

(6.24)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(\varrho)\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a_{f,\varrho})(\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H)=\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho})(H),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho}(t,x,\eta):=\frac{1}{1+|k|^{2}}p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))a_{f}(t,x,\eta).

Proof.

We have the exact composition formula

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(\varrho)\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a_{f})(\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon,f,\varrho})(H),

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon,f,\varrho}(t,x,\eta)

\displaystyle:=p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))a_{f}(t,x,\eta)\frac{1}{1+|\varepsilon k|^{2}},

since we are composing on the right by a Fourier multiplier. Since
a_{f,\varrho}
is homogeneous of degree
0
in the variable
\eta
(see Proposition
6.10
), we have

\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon,f,\varrho}(t,x,\eta)=p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))a_{f,\varrho}(t,x,\varepsilon\eta)\frac{1}{1+|\varepsilon k|^{2}}=\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho}(t,x,\varepsilon\eta),

and the conclusion follows.
∎

In the following proposition, we show that we can choose an extension of
(f,\varrho,u)
as in Subsection
6.3
and such that it satisfies a Penrose condition for all times. By definition of
T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
, we know that

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}],\ \ \underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\inf}\,|1-\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)|\geq c_{0}/2,

where we recall the expression of the Penrose symbol:

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\varrho(t,x)}{1-\rho_{f}(t,x)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(t,x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s.

We want this condition to be true for the extension of
(f,\varrho)
.
We have the following result, which requires the technical assumption (
1.2
) on the pressure.

Proposition 6.18
.

There exists
\delta^{\star}=\delta(c_{0},M_{\mathrm{in}})>0
small enough such that, considering the extension of
f
and
\varrho
with respect to
T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
and
\delta^{\star}
, we have

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\inf}\,|1-\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)|\geq c_{0}/4.

Proof.

We only treat the case of
t\in(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},+\infty)
, the case of negative times being identical. Let us set

\displaystyle\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t):=\chi_{\delta}(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}),\ \ \underline{\chi}^{\star}(t):=\underline{\chi}(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}),

where we use the notations of Section
6.3
. By definition of the extension for
f
, we have

(6.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}1-\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}&=1-\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\varrho(t,x)}{1-\rho_{f}(t,x)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad-\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\varrho(t,x)}{1-\rho_{f}(t,x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{f}}\frac{(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}\partial_{t}^{k}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

We next proceed to the following decompositions: we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\rho_{f}(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})-\chi^{\star}(t)\sum_{k=1}^{N_{f}}\partial_{t}^{k}\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\frac{(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})^{k}}{k!}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}+\frac{1}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}\frac{Q^{\star}(t)}{1-Q^{\star}(t)},

where

\displaystyle Q^{\star}(t):=\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\sum_{k=1}^{N_{f}}\partial_{t}^{k}\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\frac{(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})^{k}}{k!}}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})},

and by writing

\displaystyle\varrho(t)=\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})+R^{\star}(t),\ \ R^{\star}(t):=\chi_{1}(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\varrho}}\varrho^{(k)}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\frac{(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})^{k}}{k!},

we also have

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(\varrho(t))\varrho(t)

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\right)\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})+S^{\star}(t),

where

\displaystyle S^{\star}(t):=\Big[p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})+R^{\star}(t)\right)-p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\right)\Big]\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})+p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})+R^{\star}(t)\right)R^{\star}(t).

Note that

\displaystyle S^{\star}(t)=\underset{t\rightarrow T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}}{\mathcal{O}}(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}).

Now, the equality (
6.25
) turns into

\displaystyle 1-\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\right)\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}\frac{1}{1+|k|^{2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\mathfrak{E}^{\star}(t),

with a remainder

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}^{\star}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\Bigg(\frac{p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})\right)\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}\frac{Q^{\star}(t)}{1-Q^{\star}(t)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{S^{\star}(t)}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{S^{\star}(t)}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})}\frac{Q^{\star}(t)}{1-Q^{\star}(t)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\varrho(t,x)}{1-\rho_{f}(t,x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{f}}\frac{(t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}\partial_{t}^{k}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigg).

We claim that an homogeneity argument shows that

\displaystyle\underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\inf}\,\left|1-\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)\right)\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}\frac{1}{1+|k|^{2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle=\underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\inf}\,\left|1-\frac{p^{\prime}\left(\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)\right)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}{1-\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}\frac{1}{1+|k|^{2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s\right|\geq c_{0}/2.

Indeed, we know from Lemma
6.10
that for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the function

(\gamma,\tau,k)\mapsto\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s

is homogeneous of degree
0
, and since by Assumption (
1.2
) and by construction we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)p^{\prime}\left(\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)\right)\underline{\chi}^{\star}(t)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}{1-\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}\leq\frac{p^{\prime}\left(\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)\right)\varrho(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)}{1-\rho_{f}(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},x)},

we can rely on the factor
(1+|k|^{2})^{-1}
to show that the two infima are equal. By writing

\displaystyle\underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\inf}\,|1-\mathscr{P}_{f(t),\varrho(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)|\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2}-\underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\sup}|\mathfrak{E}^{\star}(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)|

thanks to the triangular inequality, it remains to prove that a suitable choice of
\delta
can lead to

\displaystyle\forall t\in[T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},+\infty),\ \ \underset{(x,\gamma,\tau,k)}{\sup}\,|\mathfrak{E}^{\star}(t,x,\gamma,\tau,k)|\leq\frac{c_{0}}{4}.

First note that the remainder
\mathfrak{E}^{\star}
has a factor
\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)
in factor of all the terms in its expression, and is therefore compactly supported in time, with support in
(T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}+\delta)
. Relying on the bounds for
f
and
\varrho
depending only on
M_{\mathrm{in}}
(see Remarks
2.21
and Lemma
2.22
), we can proceed as in the proof of the estimates (
6.18
)–(
6.19
)–(
6.20
) and show that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{E}^{\star}(t)|\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})\chi^{\star}_{\delta}(t)|t-T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}|\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})\delta.

This procedure is allowed since the extension and the remainder
\mathfrak{E}^{\star}
only involve a finite number of derivatives in time of
\varrho
and
f
at
t=T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
. Choosing
\delta
small enough is now sufficient to conclude.
∎

We are now in position to provide the key
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T,\mathrm{L}^{2})
estimates for a solution to the equation (
6.23
).

Proposition 6.19
.

Assume that
H
is a solution of the equation (
6.23
) on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. There exists
\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})>0
such that for any
\gamma\geq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})
, we have

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

Proof.

Thanks to (
6.24
) and Lemma
6.15
, the equation (
6.23
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho})\right)(H)=\mathcal{R},

where
\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho}
has been defined in (
6.24
). Now observe that, recalling the definition (
1.3
) of the Penrose symbol
\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathfrak{a}_{f,\varrho}=\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho},

therefore
H
satisfies

(6.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(1-\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho})(H)=\mathcal{R}.

Relying on Proposition
6.18
on the Penrose condition satisfied by the (extension) of
(f(t),\varrho(t))
on
\mathbb{R}
, we can consider

\displaystyle c_{f,\varrho}:=\frac{1}{1-\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}}.

Note that the symbol
c_{f,\varrho}-1
vanishes outside a compact set in time and hence, in view of the estimates (
6.18
)–(
6.19
)–(
6.20
) of Corollary
6.13
on the symbol
\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}
and the Faà di Bruno’s formula, we get

(6.27)

\displaystyle\omega[c_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}-1]+\Omega\left[c_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}-1\right]\leq\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}}).

Applying
\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\left(c_{f,\varrho}-1\right)
to the previous equation (
6.26
) yields

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\left(c_{f,\varrho}-1\right)(\mathcal{R})+\Big[\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\big((c_{f,\varrho}-1)(1-\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho})\big)-\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\left(c_{f,\varrho}-1\right)\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(1-\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho})\Big](H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\left(c_{f,\varrho}-1\right)(\mathcal{R})-\Big[\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\big((c_{f,\varrho}-1)\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}\big)-\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}\left(c_{f,\varrho}-1\right)\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho})\Big](H).

Using the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
continuity property from Theorem
C.2
and the commutation estimates from Proposition
C.3
in the Appendix, we get for all
\gamma>0
and
\mathrm{M}>1+2d

\displaystyle\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\omega[c_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}-1]\left\|\mathcal{R}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\frac{C}{\gamma}\Omega[c_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}-1]\Xi\big[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}\big]_{\mathrm{M}}\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})},

for some constant
C>0
depending only on the dimension. By homogeneity of the previous seminorms with respect to the semiclassical quantification in
\varepsilon
(in particular the fact that
\Omega[c_{f,\varrho}^{\varepsilon}-1]\leq\Omega[c_{f,\varrho}-1]
for
\varepsilon\leq 1
) , we infer

\displaystyle\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\omega[c_{f,\varrho}-1]\left\|\mathcal{R}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\frac{C}{\gamma}\Omega[c_{f,\varrho}-1]\big[\mathscr{P}_{f,\varrho}\big]_{\mathrm{M}}\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

Thanks to (
6.27
) and the estimate (
6.19
) of Corollary
6.13
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}})\left\|\mathcal{R}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\frac{C}{\gamma}\Lambda(1+M_{\mathrm{in}})\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

Taking
\gamma
large enough with respect to
M_{\mathrm{in}}
allows to perform an absorption argument: we get the existence of some
\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})>0
such that for any
\gamma\geq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})
, we have

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(R\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})},

which was the desired conclusion.
∎

As a consequence, we can eventually obtain an estimate for the equation (
6.21
) on
(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Corollary 6.20
.

Consider
\widetilde{H}
the solution to (
6.21
) on
(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq\Lambda(T,M_{\mathrm{in}})\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}.

Proof.

First recall that

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(t,x):=e^{\gamma t}H(t,x),\ \ \widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(t,x)=e^{\gamma t}\mathcal{R}(t,x),

and that
\mathcal{R}
has been set to
0
outside
[0,T]
. By Lemma
6.14
and Proposition
6.19
, we thus get for all
\gamma\geq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2\gamma t}\|\widetilde{H}(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\|H\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})^{2}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})^{2}\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2\gamma t}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

therefore taking
\gamma=\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})
provides

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq e^{\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})T}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us conclude this section by giving a proof of the causality principle which was stated in Lemma
6.14
.

Proof of Lemma
6.14
.

Observe that thanks to the equation satisfied by
\widetilde{H}_{1}
and
\widetilde{H}_{2}
, having
\widetilde{H}_{1}|_{(-\infty;0]}=\widetilde{H}_{2}|_{(-\infty;0]}=0
implies
\widetilde{S}_{1}|_{(-\infty;0]}=\widetilde{S}_{2}|_{(-\infty;0]}=0
.

Let
H:=e^{-\gamma t}\left(\widetilde{H}_{1}-\widetilde{H}_{2}\right)
and
S:=e^{-\gamma t}\left(\widetilde{S}_{1}-\widetilde{S}_{2}\right)
for some
\gamma>0
, which satisfy

H(t,x)-\frac{\varrho}{1-\rho_{f}}e^{-\gamma t}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\Big[e^{\gamma\bullet}\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H\Big](t,x)=S,\ \ \quad H|_{(-\infty;0]}=0,\ \ \,S|_{(-\infty;T]}=0.

By Proposition
6.19
, there exists
\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})
such that for all
\gamma\geq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})

\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2\gamma t}\left\|\widetilde{H}_{1}(t)-\widetilde{H}_{2}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\|H\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})^{2}\|S\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2},

for some
\gamma_{0}>0
and
C_{0}\geq 0
, therefore

\int_{0}^{T}e^{-2\gamma t}\left\|\widetilde{H}_{1}(t)-\widetilde{H}_{2}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})^{2}\int_{T}^{+\infty}e^{-2\gamma t}\left\|\widetilde{S}_{1}(t)-\widetilde{S}_{2}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

since
\widetilde{S}_{1}|_{(-\infty;T]}=\widetilde{S}_{2}|_{(-\infty;T]}
. We thus infer

\int_{0}^{T}\|H(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}})^{2}\int_{T}^{+\infty}e^{2\gamma(T-t)}\|S(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

By letting
\gamma\to+\infty
, we deduce that
H|_{[0,T]}=0
, hence the result.
∎

6.6.
Final hyperbolic estimates

To conclude this section, it remains to perform an energy estimate on the hyperbolic part
\left(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)(h)
.

Let us observe that by Remark
2.25
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{\infty})}\lesssim\left(1+T^{1/2}\Lambda(T,R)\right)M_{\mathrm{in}}+T^{1/2}\Lambda(T,R),

by Sobolev embedding (since
m>1+d/2
), therefore for all
t\in(0,T^{\star}_{\varepsilon})
, there holds

(6.28)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,t;\mathrm{L}^{\infty})}\leq 1+2M_{\mathrm{in}}.

We can therefore state the following lemma.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
T\in\left(0,\min\left(T_{\varepsilon}(R),\overline{T}(R),\widetilde{T}_{0}(R),\widehat{T}(R)\right)\right)
.
Assume that
h
is a solution of the equation

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)(h)=\widetilde{H},\ \ t\in[0,T],

There holds

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq e^{(1+M_{\mathrm{in}})T}T^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|h(0)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\widetilde{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\right).

Proof.

The proof is standard; the hyperbolic nature of the equation provides pointwise in time
\mathrm{L}^{2}
bounds thanks to an energy estimate (in the same spirit as the proof of Proposition
2.3
). For all
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\|h(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\leq e^{\|\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,t;\mathrm{L}^{\infty})}t}\|h(0)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\|\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,t;\mathrm{L}^{\infty})}(t-\tau)}\|\widetilde{H}(\tau)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

By (
6.28
), we get by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in time that for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\|h(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\leq e^{(1+M_{\mathrm{in}})T}\left(\|h(0)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\widetilde{H}(\tau)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\right),

hence the conclusion, eventually taking the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm in time on
(0,T)
in the previous inequality .
∎

Gathering the results of Lemma
6.2
, Proposition
6.1
, Corollary
6.20
and Lemma
6.21
, we directly infer the following statement.

Corollary 6.22
.

For all
|\alpha|\leq m
, for all
T\in\left(0,\min\left(T_{\varepsilon}(R),\overline{T}(R),\widetilde{T}_{0}(R),\widehat{T}(R)\right)\right)
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq T^{\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R).

7.
End of the proof

7.1.
Conclusion of the bootstrap

Let us conclude the bootstrap argument.
We choose

\displaystyle T\in\left(0,\min\left(T_{\varepsilon}(R),\overline{T}(R),\widetilde{T}_{0}(R),\widehat{T}(R)\right)\right).

We can consider the following explicit quantity, which appears in all the estimates from Section
6
:

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{in}}:=\left\|f^{\mathrm{in}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{r}}+\left\|\varrho^{\mathrm{in}}\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m+1}}+\left\|u^{\mathrm{in}}\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}.

We now use Corollary
6.22
to get

\displaystyle\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})}\leq T^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R).

We also invoke the energy estimate of Lemma
2.22
on
f
that yields

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\left(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}\right)}\leq M_{\mathrm{in}}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}\Lambda\left(T,R\right),

and the energy estimate of Proposition
2.24
on
u
giving

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})}

\displaystyle\leq M_{\mathrm{in}}+T^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R)+\Lambda(T,R)\|\varrho\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})},

and therefore, using the previous estimate on
\varrho
, there holds

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})}

\displaystyle\leq M_{\mathrm{in}}+T^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R).

Combining all these estimates together, we finally obtain the key estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f,\varrho,u,T)\leq C\left(M_{\mathrm{in}}+T^{1/4}\Lambda(T,R)+T^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R)\right),

for some universal constant
C>0
. Note that the previous r.h.s is independent of
\varepsilon
. Next, we choose
R
large enough so that

\displaystyle CM_{\mathrm{in}}<\frac{1}{2}R.

Now, with
R
being fixed, we use the continuity at
0
of the function

\displaystyle s\mapsto s^{1/4}\Lambda(s,R)+s^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},s,R)

to find some time
T^{\sharp}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
with

\displaystyle T^{\sharp}\in\left(0,\min\left(\overline{T}(R),\widetilde{T}_{0}(R),\widehat{T}(R)\right)\right),

and such that for every
T\in[0,T^{\sharp}]

\displaystyle C\left(T^{1/4}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R)+T^{1/2}\Lambda(M_{\mathrm{in}},T,R)\right)<\frac{1}{2}R.

We deduce that for all
T\in[0,\min(T^{\sharp},T_{\varepsilon}(R))]
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f,\varrho,u,T)<R
. In addition, thanks to Lemmas
2.16
–
2.17
, we easily get the fact that the condition (
B

(
T
)

Θ

μ
,
θ

) is satisfied, up to reducing
T^{\sharp}
so that
\Theta+T^{\sharp}R<1
and

\displaystyle T^{\sharp}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{R}\min\left(\frac{1-\Theta}{2},\ln(2),\ln\left(\frac{2\underline{\theta}}{\mu}\right)\right)\right).

Since we were assuming that
\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f,\varrho,u,T_{\varepsilon}(R))=R
, this shows that we must have
T_{\varepsilon}>T^{\sharp}
.

In conclusion, we have found
R>0
and
T>0
such that for all
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f,\varrho,u,T)\leq R.

7.2.
Existence of a solution

Let us first focus on the existence part of Theorem
1.5
. We will rely on a standard compactness argument, that we briefly detail. In view of Section
7.1
, there exist
T>0
,
R>
and

(f_{\varepsilon},\varrho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m}_{r})\times\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})

a solution to (

S
ε

) with initial data
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},u^{\mathrm{in}})
such that

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}{\sup}\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f_{\varepsilon},\varrho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},T)\leq R.

Hence, we deduce that
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})
,
(\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})
and
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})
and in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})
. From Lemma
2.20
, we also know that
(\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m-2})
. We deduce that
(f_{\varepsilon},\varrho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
has a weak(-
\star
) limit
(f,\varrho,u)
(up to some extraction) in the previous spaces.

Furthermore, using the equations on
f_{\varepsilon}
,
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
and
u_{\varepsilon}
, we know that
(\partial_{t}f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-3}_{r-1})
,
(\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m-3})
and
(\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m-3})
. Invoking the Aubin-Lions-Simon lemma (see e.g.
[
BF12
, Theorem II.5.16]
), we deduce that, up to some extraction, we have

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}f\ \ \text{in}\ \mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-2}_{r-1}),\ \ \ \varrho_{\varepsilon}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\varrho\ \ \text{in}\ \mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m-3}),\ \ \ u_{\varepsilon}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}u\ \ \text{in}\ \mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m-1}).

These strong convergences allow us to pass to the limit in (

S
ε

) and to obtain the fact that
(f,\varrho,u)
is a solution to the thick spray equations (
TS
) on
[0,T]
, and that
(f,\varrho)
satisfies a Penrose stability condition (
P
) on the same interval of time.

It remains to prove the fact that
f\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})
and
u\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m})
, as we only have

f\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})
and
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})

for the moment.
Since
f\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})\cap\mathscr{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-2}_{r-1})
and
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathscr{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m-1})
, we know (see e.g.
[
BF12
, Lemma II.5.9]
) that
f\in\mathscr{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})
and
u\in\mathscr{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m})
. It is now sufficient to prove that
t\mapsto\left\|f(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}}
and
t\mapsto\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}
are continuous functions on
[0,T]
to conclude. Coming back to the energy estimates of Subsection
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|f(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}}^{2}<+\infty,\ \ \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}<+\infty.

Since
t\mapsto\left\|f(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}}^{2}
and
t\mapsto\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}
are integrable (because
f\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})
and
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})
), we obtain the fact that
t\mapsto\left\|f(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}}^{2}
and
t\mapsto\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}
belongs to
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}(0,T)
, hence the continuity in time of these quantities.
This finally yields the desired continuity for
f
and
u
and concludes the proof.

7.3.
Uniqueness of the solution

Let us turn to the uniqueness part of Theorem
1.5
. Let
(f_{1},\varrho_{1},u_{1})
and
(f_{2},\varrho_{2},u_{2})
be two solutions of (
TS
) belonging to

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\bm{T};\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})\times\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m})\times\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m+1}),

for some
\bm{T}>0
, with the same initial condition
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},u^{\mathrm{in}})
and such that
t\mapsto(f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t))
satisfies the Penrose stability condition (
P
) on
[0,\bm{T}]
. Let us set

\displaystyle f:=f_{1}-f_{2},\ \ \alpha_{i}:=1-\rho_{f_{i}},\ \ \alpha:=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2},\ \ \varrho:=\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2},\ \ u:=u_{1}-u_{2},

and

\displaystyle R:=\underset{i=1,2}{\max}\left(\left\|f_{i}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\bm{T};\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r})}+\left\|\varrho_{i}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m})}+\left\|u_{i}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\bm{T};\mathrm{H}^{m+1}),}\right).

Note that
R
depends on
\bm{T}
.

\bullet

Step 1
: let us show that
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
, at least on a small interval of time. The key is to obtain
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})
estimates for
\varrho
for some
T=T(R)\leq\bm{T}
. To this end, we write the equation satisfied by
\varrho
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\left(\partial_{t}\varrho+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho\right)+\varrho_{1}\left(\partial_{t}\alpha+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=-\Bigg(\partial_{t}\varrho_{2}\alpha+\partial_{t}\alpha_{2}\varrho+[\varrho u_{1}+\varrho_{2}u]\cdot\nabla_{x}\alpha_{2}+[\alpha u_{1}+\alpha_{2}u]\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho_{2}+\alpha\rho_{1}\mathrm{div}_{x}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}\varrho\mathrm{div}_{x}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}\varrho_{2}\mathrm{div}_{x}u\Bigg),

and since
\partial_{t}\alpha_{i}=\mathrm{div}_{x}\,j_{f_{i}}
, we get

(7.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho+\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\mathrm{div}_{x}(j_{f}-\rho_{f}u_{1})=S_{1,2}(f,\varrho,u),

where

(7.2)

S_{1,2}(f,\varrho,u):=-\frac{1}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\Bigg(-\partial_{t}\varrho_{2}\rho_{f}+\partial_{t}\alpha_{2}\varrho+[\varrho u_{1}+\varrho_{2}u]\cdot\nabla_{x}\alpha_{2}+[-\rho_{f}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}u]\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho_{2}\\
-\rho_{f}\rho_{1}\mathrm{div}_{x}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}\varrho\mathrm{div}_{x}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}\varrho_{2}\mathrm{div}_{x}u-\varrho_{2}\rho_{f}\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u_{1}\Bigg).

The r.h.s
S_{1,2}(f,\varrho,u)
can be seen as a source term whose
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})
norm will be estimated by that of
\varrho
, without any loss of derivative in
(f,\varrho,u)
. Note that we have rewritten the equation as above in order to perform the right pseudodifferential factorization. It is of course reminiscent of what we have done in Section
6
. Next, the equations on the differences
f
and
u
read

(7.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathrm{div}_{v}\big[(u_{2}-v)f-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2})f\big]+\Big(u-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{1})+\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2})\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}=0,\\[5.69054pt]
&\partial_{t}u+(u_{2}\cdot\nabla_{x})u+(u\cdot\nabla_{x})u_{1}+\frac{1}{\varrho_{1}}\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{1})-\frac{1}{\varrho_{2}}\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\varrho_{1}}\Big(\Delta_{x}+\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}\Big)u-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\varrho_{1}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}\varrho_{2}}\right)\Big(\Delta_{x}+\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}\Big)u_{2}=j_{f}-\rho_{f}u_{1}-\rho_{f_{2}}u,\end{aligned}\right.

and in particular

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathrm{div}_{v}\big[(u_{2}-v)f-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2})f\big]\\[2.84526pt]
=p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1})\nabla_{x}\varrho\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}+(p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1})-p^{\prime}(\varrho_{2}))\nabla_{x}\varrho_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}-u\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{1}.

Again, the last two terms in the r.h.s should be seen as some source terms, without any loss of derivative in
(f,\varrho,u)
. One can show that

\displaystyle\left\|(p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1})-p^{\prime}\varrho_{2}))\nabla_{x}\varrho_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}+\left\|u\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}.

The proof of the estimate for the second term will be similar to the one for the term
S[\varrho]
we will treat below.

Arguing as in Sections
3
–
4
–
5
, but for a force field

\mathfrak{F}(t,x):=u_{2}(t,x)-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2}(t,x)),

instead of
E^{\varrho,u}_{\mathrm{reg},\varepsilon}
, one can also prove that for
T(R)
small enough, we have for all
t\in[0,T(R)]

\displaystyle\rho_{f}(t,x)

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1}(t,x))\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{x}\varrho(s,x-(t-s)v)\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{R}[\rho_{f}](t,x),

\displaystyle j_{f}(t,x)

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1}(t,x))\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v\nabla_{x}\varrho(s,x-(t-s)v)\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{1}(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{R}[j_{f}](t,x),

where

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{R}[\rho_{f}]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1})}+\left\|\mathcal{R}[j_{f}]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1})}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}.

Here, we have also used the fact that
f_{\mid t=0}=0
. Injecting these expressions in equation (
7.1
) on
\varrho
, we get as in Section
6

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho+\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\mathrm{div}_{x}\Big[\mathrm{K}_{1,G_{1}}^{\mathrm{free}}(\varrho)-\mathrm{K}_{G_{1}}^{\mathrm{free}}(\varrho)u_{1}\Big]=S[\varrho],

with

G_{1}(t,x,v):=p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1}(t,x))\nabla_{v}f_{1}(t,x,v),

and

S[\varrho]=-\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\mathrm{div}_{x}\left(\mathcal{R}[j_{f}]-\mathcal{R}[\rho_{f}]u_{1}\right)+S_{1,2}(f,\varrho,u),

(the operator
\mathrm{K}_{1,G_{1}}^{\mathrm{free}}
being defined in (
6.2
)) and then

(7.4)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{free}}_{G_{1}}\right)\Big[\partial_{t}\varrho+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho\Big]=S[\varrho],\ \ t\in(0,T).

It is straightforward to prove that the source
S[\varrho]
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|S[\varrho]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}.

The main issue comes from obtaining the same estimate for the term
S_{1,2}(u,f)
defined in (
7.2
). We observe that it is made of a sum of three types of terms, of the following form:

-

some terms where
\varrho
is in factor, so that we directly obtain the estimate;

-

some terms where
\rho_{f}
is in factor: we can rely on the previous decomposition of
\rho_{f}
as
\rho_{f}=\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{free}}_{G_{1}}[\varrho]+\mathcal{R}[\rho_{f}]
and an
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})
estimate is then provided by the smoothing estimate of Proposition
4.4
;

-

some terms where
u
or
\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u
is in factor: to estimate them, we perfom the same kind of energy estimate as in Subsection
B
of the Appendix, following the steps leading to the equality (
B.4
), which gives after integration in time (since
u_{\mid t=0}=0
)

\displaystyle\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|u(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|\rho_{f}(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|j_{f}(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|(\alpha_{1}\varrho_{1}(s)-\alpha_{2}\varrho_{2}(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|u(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}.

Here, we have also used the smoothing estimate from Proposition
4.4
combined with the previous decomposition of
\rho_{f}
and
j_{f}
. We obtain the desired control on
u
after an application of Grönwall’s lemma.

As in Subsection
6.5
, we then study the equation

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{free}}_{G_{1}}\right)\left[\widetilde{H}\right]=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\ \ 0\leq t\leq T,

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
is a given source term and we want to derive an
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})
estimate on the solution
\widetilde{H}
. After applying the same extension procedure for the coefficients (depending on
(f_{1},\varrho_{1},u_{1})
) in the equation as in Section
6.3
, and by setting
\widetilde{H}(t,x):=e^{\gamma t}H(t,x)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(t,x)=e^{\gamma t}\mathcal{R}(t,x)
for
\gamma>0
(with the same continuation by zero outside
[0,T]
as in Subsection
6.5
), we are led to the study of the pseudodifferential equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(1-\mathcal{P}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}})(H)=\mathcal{R},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k):=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1}(x))\varrho_{1}(x)}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}(x)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}ik\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f_{1}\right)(t,x,ks)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Observe that there is no factor
(1+|k|^{2})^{-1}
in the definition of this symbol, because there is no regularization operator in the equation. Note also that the estimates (
6.15
)–(
6.16
)–(
6.17
) holds true for
\mathcal{P}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}
, in view of the regularity of
(f_{1},\varrho_{1})
. Likewise, as in Corollary
6.20
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))},

provided that one can apply
\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathcal{P}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}}\right)
to the previous pseudodifferential equation and take
\gamma
large enough in order to invert it up to a small remainder. To do so, we prove that if the condition (
P
)

(
P
)

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ \ \underset{(\gamma,\tau,k)\in(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}}{\inf}\,|1-\mathscr{P}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)|>c,

holds for some
c>0
, then the condition

(
Opt-P
)

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ \ \underset{(\gamma,\tau,k)\in(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}}{\inf}\,|1-\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)|>c,

holds as well. Here, we implicitly consider the extension in time of
f_{1}
and
\varrho_{1}
, as it was done in Section
6.3
. To this end, we rely on a homogeneity argument, as in
[
HKR16
]
: we define the function

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}(x,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k},\sigma):=\mathscr{P}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}(x,\sigma\widetilde{\gamma},\sigma\widetilde{\tau},\sigma\widetilde{k}),\ \ \ x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ (\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k})\in S^{+},\ \sigma>0,

where

\displaystyle S^{+}:=\left\{(\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k})\in(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}\mid\widetilde{\gamma}^{2}+\widetilde{\tau}^{2}+\widetilde{k}^{2}=1\right\}.

Since
f_{1}
is regular enough, one can prove that
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}
can be extended as a continuous function on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times S^{+}\times[0,+\infty)
and we obtain

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ \ \underset{(\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k},\sigma)\in S^{+}\times[0,+\infty)}{\inf}\,|1-\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k},\sigma)|>c.

In view of the homogeneity of degree
0
of the symbol
a_{f,\varrho}
with respect to the variable
(\gamma,\tau,\eta)
(see Lemma
6.17
), we also have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{f_{1},\varrho_{1}}(x,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k},\sigma)=\frac{1}{1+\sigma^{2}|\widetilde{k}|^{2}}\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho_{1}(x))\varrho_{1}(x)}{1-\rho_{f_{1}}(x)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\widetilde{\gamma}+i\widetilde{\tau})s}i\widetilde{k}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f_{1}\right)(t,x,\widetilde{k}s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k},0)=\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{k}),

from which we infer that (
Opt-P
) holds on
S^{+}
. Again, the homogeneity of degree
0
of
\mathscr{P}_{f_{1}(t),\varrho_{1}(t)}(x,\gamma,\tau,k)
with respect to the variable
(\gamma,\tau,k)
implies that
(
Opt-P
)
holds.

Next, we come up with the transport equation on
\varrho

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varrho=\widetilde{H},

where
\varrho_{\mid t=0}=0
and where the source term
\widetilde{H}
has been shown to satisfied

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|S[\varrho]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}.

Performing an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
energy estimate gives for all
t\in[0,T]
(since
\varrho_{\mid t=0}=0
)

\displaystyle\|\varrho(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\leq\Lambda(T,R)\int_{0}^{t}\|\widetilde{H}(\tau)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\leq T^{\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))},

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. We end up with

\displaystyle\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq T\Lambda(T,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}.

We deduce the fact that there exists a small enough
T=T(R)>0
which depends only on
R
such that

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T(R)],\ \ \varrho(t)=0.

\bullet

Step 2
: let us now prove that
f_{1}=f_{2}
and
u_{1}=u_{2}
on
[0,T(R)]
. It is in fact a direct consequence of
\varrho=\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2}=0
on
[0,T(R)]
. Indeed, the previous step has shown that for all
t\in[0,T(R)]

\displaystyle\left\|u(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|u(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\Lambda(t,R)\left\|\varrho\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,t;\mathrm{L}^{2})}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|u(s)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

therefore we directly have
u=0
on
[0,T(R)]
. The equations on (
7.3
) on
f
now turn into

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-v\cdot\nabla_{v}f+\big(u_{2}-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{2})\big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f=df,\\[5.69054pt]
f_{\mid t=0}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

The method of characteristics thus shows that
f=0
on
[0,T(R)]
.

In conclusion, we have obtained
(f,\varrho,u)=(0,0,0)
on
[0,T(R)]
. We eventually observe that we can repeat this procedure starting from
t=T(R)
instead of
t=0
. As a matter of fact,
f_{1}(T(R),\cdot)
still satisfies the Penrose stability condition. Since the time
T(R)
only depends on
R
, we obtain
\varrho=0
on
[0,2T(R)]
and then
(f,u)=(0,0)
on
[0,2T(R)]
. After a finite number of steps, this yields
(f,\varrho,u)=(0,0,0)
on
[0,\bm{T}]
. This finally concludes the proof of the uniqueness part of the statement.

8.
Generalization to the non-barotropic case

In this section, we show how to handle the case of the full Navier-Stokes system for non-barotropic fluids, where we consider the additional internal energy
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
for the fluid and where the the pressure depends on
\varrho
and
\mathfrak{e}
. As explained in the introduction, the system which is at stake is the following:

(8.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+{\rm div}_{v}\Big[f(u-v)-f\nabla_{x}p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})\Big]&=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u)&=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u\otimes u)+\alpha\nabla_{x}p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})-\Delta_{x}u-\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}u&=j_{f}-\rho_{f}u,\\
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho\mathfrak{e})+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho\mathfrak{e}u)+p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})\left(\partial_{t}\alpha+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha u)\right)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u-v|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v,\\
\alpha&=1-\rho_{f}.\end{aligned}\right.

Here, we will assume
4
4

4

It is likely that more general pressure
p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})
could be treated by our method.
that the pressure law is given as

\displaystyle p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})=\pi(\varrho\mathfrak{e}),

for some given function
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}{\setminus}\{0\})
. For instance, the relation
p(\varrho,\mathfrak{e})=b\varrho\mathfrak{e}
(with
b>0
) is a perfect gas pressure law.
Similarly to the technical hypothesis (
1.2
), we shall assume that

\displaystyle y\mapsto\pi^{\prime}(y)(y+\pi(y))\ \ \text{is nondecreasing on}\ \ \mathbb{R}.

Setting

\displaystyle\vartheta:=\varrho\mathfrak{e},

the system in
(f,\varrho,u,\vartheta)
can be rewritten as

(TS
e
)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathrm{div}_{v}\big[f(u-v-\nabla_{x}\pi(\vartheta))\big]&=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u)&=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u\otimes u)+\alpha\nabla_{x}\pi(\vartheta)-\Delta_{x}u-\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}u&=j_{f}-\rho_{f}u,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}(\alpha\vartheta)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\vartheta u)+\pi(\vartheta)\left(\partial_{t}\alpha+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha u)\right)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v-u|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v,\\
\alpha&=1-\rho_{f}.\end{aligned}\right.

As we shall see below, it is significant to define the following Penrose symbol of a function
(f(x,v),\vartheta(x))
as

(8.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{f,\vartheta}^{\mathrm{energy}}(x,\gamma,\tau,\eta):=\frac{\pi^{\prime}(\vartheta(x))\left[\vartheta(x)+\pi(\vartheta(x))\right]}{1-\rho_{f}(x)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-(\gamma+i\tau)s}\frac{ik}{1+|k|^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathcal{F}_{v}\nabla_{v}f\right)(x,s\eta)\,\mathrm{d}s.

We can now introduce the following Penrose condition, adapted to (
TSe
), which is that there exists
c>0
such that

(
P
energy
)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ \ \underset{(\gamma,\tau,\eta)\in(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}{\setminus}\{0\}}{\inf}\,|1-\mathbf{P}_{f,\vartheta}^{\mathrm{energy}}(x,\gamma,\tau,\eta)|>c,

Our main result for the system (
TSe
) reads as follows.

Theorem 8.1
.

There exist
m_{0}>0
and
r_{0}>0
, depending only on the dimension, such that the following holds for all
m\geq m_{0}
and
r\geq r_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle f^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{H}^{m}_{r},\ \ \varrho^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathrm{H}^{m-1},\ \ u^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathrm{H}^{m},\ \ \vartheta^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathrm{H}^{m+1},

such that
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\vartheta^{\mathrm{in}})
satisfies the
c
-Penrose stability condition
\eqref{cond:Penrose-energyintern}_{c}
(with
c>0
) and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq f^{\mathrm{in}},\ \ \ \rho_{f^{\mathrm{in}}}<\Upsilon<1,\ \ \ 0<\mu\leq\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},\vartheta^{\mathrm{in}}\ \ \ 0<\underline{\nu}\leq(1-\rho_{f^{\mathrm{in}}})\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},(1-\rho_{f^{\mathrm{in}}})\vartheta^{\mathrm{in}}\leq\overline{\nu},

for some fixed constants
\Upsilon,\mu,\underline{\nu},\overline{\nu}
.
Then there exist
T>0
and a solution
(f,\varrho,u,\vartheta)
to (
TSe
) with initial condition
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},u^{\mathrm{in}},\vartheta^{\mathrm{in}})
such that

\displaystyle f\in\mathscr{C}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}\right),\ \ \varrho\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m-1}),\ \ \ u\in\mathscr{C}\left([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m}\right)\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1}),\ \ \vartheta\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m}),

and with
(f(t),\vartheta(t))
satisfying the
c/2
-Penrose stability condition
\eqref{cond:Penrose-energyintern}_{c/2}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. In addition, this solution is unique in this class.

Let us explain the main strategy for the proof of Theorem
8.1
. First, we observe that the equation on
\vartheta
can be rewritten as

(8.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\vartheta+u\cdot\nabla_{x}\vartheta+\frac{\vartheta+\pi(\vartheta)}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{div}(j_{f}-\rho_{f}u)=-\frac{\vartheta+\pi(\vartheta)}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u+\frac{1}{1-\rho_{f}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v-u|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v.

Appart from the last term, this equation has exactly the same structure as the one for
\varrho
in Lemma
2.2
. Hence, the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\|\vartheta(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}\leq\|\varrho^{\mathrm{in}}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}\Phi\Big(T,\cdots,\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})},\|f\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m+1}_{r})}\Big),

and features the same loss of derivative as for
\varrho
in (
TS
). Note also that the equation on
\varrho
can be directly solved once
f
and
u
are given. As in Section
2.2
, we consider the regularization
-\pi^{\prime}(\vartheta)\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}\vartheta
of the term
-\nabla_{x}\pi(\vartheta)
in the kinetic equation of (
TSe
) and introduce the quantity

\textbf{N}_{m,r}(f_{\varepsilon},\varrho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon},T):=\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\left(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{r}\right)}+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m-1})}\\
+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})}+\|\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})}.

Following the bootstrap procedure we have set for the case of (
TS
), we mainly want to control the quantity
\|\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})}
.

Using (
8.3
) (and dropping the dependency in
\varepsilon
) it is possible to obtain the following equation on
h=\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\vartheta
with
|\alpha|\leq m
(see Proposition
6.1
): one has

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\vartheta+\pi(\vartheta)}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{K}_{G}^{\mathrm{free}}\circ\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\right)\Big[\partial_{t}h+u\cdot\nabla_{x}h\Big]=\mathcal{R},\ \ t\in(0,T),

with
G(t,x,v)=\pi^{\prime}(\vartheta(t,x))\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)
and

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq\Lambda(T,R,\|h(0)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}).

Following the arguments of Section
6
, we are thus led to the study of the following pseudodifferential equation

\displaystyle H-\frac{\pi^{\prime}(\vartheta)(\vartheta+\pi(\vartheta))}{1-\rho_{f}}\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma}(a_{f})(\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}H)=\mathcal{R},\ \ \text{on}\ \ (0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
a_{f}
is defined in (
6.8
), that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Op}^{\gamma,\varepsilon}(1-\mathbf{P}_{f,\vartheta}^{\mathrm{energy}})(H)=\mathcal{R}.

In particular, it explains the introduction of the Penrose symbol (
8.2
) above, which allows to invert the previous equation on
H
, up to a small remainder.

Some additional arguments have also to be given to treat the last term in (
8.3
). Since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v-u|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v+|u|^{2}\rho_{f}-2u\cdot j_{f},

we have to include the treatment of the second order moment in velocity
m_{2}f(t,x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}f(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}v
in the analysis of Section
5
. In addition to Proposition
5.1
, we have

Proposition 8.2
.

For all
|I|\leq m
, we have for any
t\in(0,T)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{I}m_{2}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}(\varrho(t,x))\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}\nabla_{x}[\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{x}^{I}\varrho](s,x-(t-s)v)\cdot\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s+R^{I}[m_{2}f](t,x),

where the remainder
R^{I}[m_{2}f]
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|R^{I}[M_{2}f]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T,\mathrm{H}^{1}_{x})}\leq\Lambda(T,R).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}^{I}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v-u|^{2}f\,\mathrm{d}v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2})}\leq\Lambda(T,R).

The fairly straightforward adaptation of the analysis of Section
5
is left to the reader.

9.
Generalization to the inelastic Boltzmann case

In this section, we consider the case where one takes into account inelastic collisions between particles. This corresponds to the following Vlasov-Boltzmann equation in the coupling (the other equations on
(\varrho,u)
being unchanged):

(TS-Coll)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+{\rm div}_{v}\big[f(u-v)-f\nabla_{x}p(\varrho)\big]&=\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(f,f),\\
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u)&=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u\otimes u)+\alpha\nabla_{x}p-\Delta_{x}u-\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u&=j_{f}-\rho_{f}u,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(f,f)
stands for a quadratic collision operator of Boltzmann-type, in a inelastic hard-spheres regime. Here, the fixed parameter
\lambda\in(0,1)
corresponds to the so-called
restitution coefficient
: if
{}^{\prime}v
and
{}^{\prime}v_{\star}
denote the velocities of two particles before collision, their respective velocities
v
and
v_{\star}
after collision are given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} v&={{}^{\prime}v}-\frac{1+\lambda}{2}({{}^{\prime}u}\cdot n)n,\\
v_{\star}&={{}^{\prime}v_{\star}}+\frac{1+\lambda}{2}({{}^{\prime}u}\cdot n)n,\end{aligned}\right.

where
{{}^{\prime}u}:={{}^{\prime}v}-{{}^{\prime}v_{\star}}
is the relative pre-collision velocity and
n\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is a unit vector that points from the particle center with velocity
v
to the particle center with velocity
v_{\star}
at the impact. Note that
\lambda=1
corresponds to the standard elastic case.

In this representation, given two distribution functions
f=f(v)
and
g=g(v)
, we can consider the following expression for Boltzmann collision operator, as a difference of a gain and loss term

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(f,g)=\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}^{+}(f,g)-\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}^{-}(f,g),

where, setting
u=v-v_{\star}
and
\widehat{u}=u/|u|
, we define

(9.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}^{+}(f,g)(v)&=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|u\cdot n|b(\widehat{u}\cdot n)f({{}^{\prime}v})g({{}^{\prime}v_{\star}})\,\mathrm{d}v_{\star}\mathrm{d}n,\\[2.84526pt]
\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}^{-}(f,g)(v)&=f(v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|u\cdot n|b(\widehat{u}\cdot n)g(v_{\star})\,\mathrm{d}v_{\star}\mathrm{d}n.\end{split}

Here, the function
b\in\mathrm{L}^{1}([-1,1])
is a given angular collision kernel. For the sake of simplicity, we consider
b\equiv 1
. Note that in the (truly) inelastic case
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle|v|^{2}+|v_{\star}|^{2}=|{{}^{\prime}v}|^{2}+|{{}^{\prime}v_{\star}}|^{2}-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2}({{}^{\prime}u}\cdot n)^{2},

thus inducing a loss of kinetic energy at each collision, while mass and momentum are conserved. We refer to
[
Vil06
]
(see also the introduction of
[
ALT20
]
) for more details on this model coming from the theory of
granular media
and which describes a cloud of macroscopic particles of size larger than that usually described by the usual Boltzmann equation with elastic collisions (for so-called molecular gases). To include dissipative effects, inelastic collisions are thus considered. Note that the presence of such a collision operator (with a large parameter
\varepsilon^{-1}
in front of it) formally leads to a biphasic fluid model when starting from (
TS-Coll
)) (see
[
DM10
]
). We also refer to
[
O’R81
,
Bar04
,
DC09
]

for its use in the study of sprays.

Our main result (which also includes the elastic case
\lambda=1
) reads as follows.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,1]
. There exist
m_{0}>0
, depending only on the dimension, such that the following holds for all
m\geq m_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle e^{|v|^{2}}f^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{m},\ \ \varrho^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathrm{H}^{m+1},\ \ u^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathrm{H}^{m},

such that
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\varrho^{\mathrm{in}})
satisfies the
c
-Penrose stability condition
\eqref{cond:Penrose}_{c}
(with
c>0
) and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq f^{\mathrm{in}}\ \ \ \rho_{f^{\mathrm{in}}}<\Theta<1,\ \ \ 0<\mu\leq\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},\ \ \ 0<\underline{\theta}\leq(1-\rho_{f^{\mathrm{in}}})\varrho^{\mathrm{in}}\leq\overline{\theta},

for some fixed constants
\Theta,\mu,\underline{\theta},\overline{\theta}
.
Then there exist
T>0
and a solution
(f,\varrho,u)
to (
TS-Coll
) with initial condition
(f^{\mathrm{in}},\varrho^{\mathrm{in}},u^{\mathrm{in}})
such that

\displaystyle e^{|v|^{2}}f\in\mathscr{C}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{m-1}_{0}\right),\ \ \varrho\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m}),\ \ u\in\mathscr{C}\left([0,T];\mathrm{H}^{m}\right)\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1}),

and with
(f(t),\varrho(t))
satisfying the
c/2
-Penrose stability condition
\eqref{cond:Penrose}_{c/2}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. In addition, this solution is unique in this class.

As seen below, the friction term in the kinetic equation comes in handy in order to treat some of the new terms due to the collision operator. This was remarked already in
[
Mat10
]
.

Let us present the main changes that must be considered in our strategy of proof and that are due to the collision operator. It mainly concerns:

-

the energy estimates for
f
from Section
2
;

-

the integro-differential system for the derivatives of
f
from Section
5.1
.

The rest of Section
2
and of Section
5
then remains unchanged, as well as Sections
6
–
7
.

9.1.
New energy estimates for the kinetic part

Let us focus on the energy estimates for the new kinetic equation

(9.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+{\rm div}_{v}[f(u-v)-f\nabla_{x}p(\varrho)f]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(f,f).

Following
[
Mat10
]
, we first define
g(t,x,v)=e^{|v|^{2}}f(t,x,v)
with
f
solving (
9.2
).
Setting

E^{u,\varrho}=u-\nabla_{x}p(\varrho),

it implies that
g
satisfies the following modified Vlasov-Boltzmann equation

(9.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g+v\cdot\nabla_{x}g+{\rm div}_{v}[(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g]-2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g=\Gamma_{\lambda}[g,g],

where for all functions
h_{1}(v),h_{2}(v)
, the operator
\Gamma_{\lambda}[g,g]=\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}[g,g]-\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}[g,g]
is defined via

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}[h_{1},h_{2}](v)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}e^{-|v_{\star}|^{2}-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2}(({{}^{\prime}v}-{{}^{\prime}v_{\star}})\cdot n)^{2}}|u\cdot n|h_{1}({{}^{\prime}v})h_{2}({{}^{\prime}v_{\star}})\,\mathrm{d}v_{\star}\mathrm{d}n,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}[h_{1},h_{2}](v)

\displaystyle:=h_{2}(v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}e^{-|v_{\star}|^{2}}|u\cdot n|h_{1}(v_{\star})\,\mathrm{d}v_{\star}\mathrm{d}n.

Note that the additional term
2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g
comes from the friction term in (
9.2
). Using

\displaystyle{{}^{\prime}u}={{}^{\prime}v_{\star}}-{{}^{\prime}v}=v_{\star}-v-(1+\lambda)({{}^{\prime}u}\cdot n)n=v_{\star}-v+(1+\lambda)\lambda(u\cdot n)n,

we observe that for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
c(\lambda)>0
(and
c(1)=0)
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}[h_{1},h_{2}]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}e^{-|v_{\star}|^{2}-c(\lambda)(({v}-{v_{\star}})\cdot n)^{2}}|(v-v_{\star})\cdot n|h_{1}({{}^{\prime}v})h_{2}({{}^{\prime}v_{\star}})\,\mathrm{d}v_{\star}\mathrm{d}n.

The exponential inside the integral is roughly behaving as
e^{-|v_{\star}|^{2}-c(\lambda)|v-v_{\star}|^{2}}
. We have the following bilinear estimates on the previous collision operators, where some loss of weights in velocity classically shows up.

Lemma 9.2
.

There exists
s=s(d)>0
large enough such that for all
\sigma\geq 0
, we have for any smooth nonnegative function
g=g(x,v)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq s}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle v\rangle^{2\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}[\Gamma_{\lambda}(g,g)]\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}g\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}v\lesssim\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\sigma}}^{2}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\sigma+1}}.

Proof.

We refer to
[
Mat10
, Lemma 2.3]
combined to the (proof of)
[
ALT20
, Appendix A]
.
∎

The key estimate allowing to recover the previous loss of weight then comes from the following lemma bearing on the extra term
2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
s\geq 0
and
\sigma>0
. For any smooth nonnegative function
g=g(x,v)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have

\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq s}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle v\rangle^{2\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}\left[2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g\right]\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}g\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}v\\
\lesssim-(1-\delta)\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\sigma+1}}^{2}+\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|E^{u,\varrho}\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s}}^{2}\right)\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\sigma}}^{2}.

Proof.

We refer to
[
Mat10
, Lemma 2.7]
.
∎

Let us show how one can now obtain an
a priori
energy estimate for a solution
g
to (
9.3
), that reads as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{u,\varrho}(g)-2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g=\Gamma_{\lambda}(g,g),

where

\mathcal{T}^{u,\varrho}=\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}-v\cdot\nabla_{v}+E^{u,\varrho}(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{v}-d\mathrm{Id}.

The result is the following.

Lemma 9.4
.

For all
r\geq 0
,
m>3+d/2
,
c>0
and all
T>0
, for all smooth functions
(f,\varrho,u)
satisfying

\partial_{t}g+v\cdot\nabla_{x}g-v\cdot\nabla_{v}g+E^{u,\varrho}(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{v}-2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g-dg=\Gamma_{\lambda}(g,g)\quad\text{on}\quad[0,T],

and
\varrho\geq c
on
[0,T]
, there holds, for all
t\in[0,T]

(9.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g(t)]&\leq\|g(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}\exp\Big[C\Big((1+\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})}+\left\|\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T)})T\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+{\sqrt{T}}\Lambda\left(\|\varrho\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m-2})}\Big)\|\varrho\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})}\right)\Big],\end{split}

for some universal constant
C>0
and where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g(t)]:=\|g(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\|g(\tau)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma+1}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.9
, we first have

\mathcal{T}^{u,\varrho}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}g)=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
\beta_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{d}\left(\partial_{x}^{\widehat{\alpha}^{i}}\partial_{v}^{\overline{\beta}^{i}}g-\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}g\right)-\left[\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta},E^{u,\varrho}(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{v}\right]g\\
+\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}\left[2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}-v)g\right]+\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}\left[\Gamma_{\lambda}(f,f)\right].

The analogue of inequality (
2.3
) from Proposition
2.10
is, thanks to Lemmas
9.2
–
9.3
, for all
\delta\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}+(1-\delta)\left\|g(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\|E^{u,\varrho}(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}+\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|E^{u,\varrho}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}\right)\|g(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}+\left\|g(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}\left\|g(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\|E^{u,\varrho}(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}+\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|E^{u,\varrho}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}\right)\|g(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{1-\delta}\left\|g(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma}}^{4}+\frac{1-\delta}{2}\left\|g(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m}_{\sigma+1}}^{2},

therefore after absorbing the last term with
\delta=1/2
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g(t)]\lesssim\left(1+\left\|E^{u,\varrho}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{m}}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g(t)]\right)\mathcal{E}_{m,\sigma}[g(t)].

Concluding as in the proof of Proposition
2.10
, we finally obtain the result.
∎

The bootstrap argument from Section
2.2
is then performed with the quantity

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{m,r}(g,\varrho,u,T):=\left\|\mathcal{E}_{m-1,\sigma}[g]\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T)}+\|\varrho\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})}+\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{m+1})},

instead of
\mathcal{N}_{m,r}(f,\varrho,u,T)
, by considering the same regularization
\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}\varrho
in the equation (
9.3
). Taking into account (
9.4
) and the quantity
\mathscr{N}_{m,r}(g,\varrho,u,T)
, the content of Section
2
can be modified accordingly. Concerning the local in time existence for the regularized system from Section
B
, we just modify the kinetic part of the scheme of approximation:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}g^{n+1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}g^{n+1}+\mathrm{div}_{v}\left[(E^{n}(t,x)-v)g^{n+1}\right]-2v\cdot(E^{u,\varrho}_{\mathrm{reg},\varepsilon}-v)g^{n+1}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\Gamma_{\lambda}^{+}[g^{n},g^{n}]-\Gamma_{\lambda}^{-}[g^{n},g^{n+1}],\\
&{f^{n+1}}_{\mid t=0}=f^{in}.\end{aligned}\right.

where
\varepsilon>0
is given. We refer to
[
Mat10
]
for more details.

9.2.
Equation on the augmented variable
\mathcal{F}

Let us highlight the main changes that arise in the end of Subsection
5.1
, more precisely in Definition
5.5
. Recall that the goal is to consider a new unknown
\mathcal{F}=\left(\partial_{x}^{K}\partial_{v}^{K}f\right)_{|K|+|L|\in\{m-1,m\}}
. Here, one has to consider a new coupling matrix
\mathbb{M}=\left(\mathbb{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}\right)
which takes into account the collision operator
\mathcal{Q}
, defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}:=\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}+\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}^{\mathcal{Q}},

with
|I|+|J|,|K|+|L|\in\{m-1,m\}
and where

-

\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}\in\mathbb{R}
stands for the former terms of the coupling matrix already appearing in Definition
5.5
;

-

\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}^{\mathcal{Q}}
is an new operator term coming from the collision operator and defined by the relation

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{I}\partial_{v}^{J}\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(f,f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq I\\
0\leq\beta\leq J\end{subarray}}\binom{I}{\alpha}\binom{J}{\beta}\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f,\partial_{x}^{I-\alpha}\partial_{v}^{J-\beta}f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|K|+|L|\in\{m-1,m\}}\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}^{\mathcal{Q}}\left[\partial_{x}^{K}\partial_{v}^{L}f\right],

that is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{(I,J),(K,L)}^{\mathcal{Q}}(\bullet):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq I\\
0\leq\beta\leq J\\
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{I}{\alpha}\binom{J}{\beta}\mathbf{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}K=I-\alpha\\
L=J-\beta\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}\Big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f,\bullet\Big).

Hence,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
is a matrix with operator coefficients, acting on
\mathcal{F}=\left(\partial_{x}^{K}\partial_{v}^{K}f\right)_{|K|+|L|\in\{m-1,m\}}
.

Using the same notations as in Section
5.1
, we obtain the following equation on
\mathcal{F}=\left(\partial_{x}^{K}\partial_{v}^{K}f\right)_{|K|+|L|\in\{m-1,m\}}
(see (
5.3
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\textnormal{reg},\varepsilon}^{u,\varrho}\mathcal{F}+\mathbb{M}\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{L}=-\mathcal{R}_{0}-\mathcal{R}_{1}.

After the composition by
(t,x,v)\mapsto(t,\mathrm{X}^{t;0}(x,v),\mathrm{V}^{t;0}(x,v))
where
\mathrm{Z}=(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{V})
is the solution to (
5.4
), there holds the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}+\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}=d\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1},

with
\widetilde{g}(t,x,v)=g(t,\mathrm{X}^{t;0}(x,v),\mathrm{V}^{t;0}(x,v))
. We can still consider the resolvant associated to the previous operator
\mathbb{M}-d\mathrm{Id}
, that is the solution
s\mapsto\mathfrak{N}^{s,t}(x,v)
of

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{s}\mathfrak{N}^{s;t}+\left[\mathbb{M}\circ\mathrm{Z}^{s;0}-d\mathrm{Id}\right]\mathfrak{N}^{s;t}&=0,\\
\mathfrak{N}^{t;t}&=\mathrm{Id},\end{aligned}\right.

whose existence and uniqueness is still provided by the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem,
Hence, this shows that the contribution of the collision operator
\mathcal{Q}
can be handled by the modified operator
\mathbb{M}
. The strategy of proof which is performed in the rest of Section
5
applies
mutatis mutandis
.

10.
Generalization to the density-dependent drag case

In this section, we show how one can deal with the case of density-dependent drag in the force acting on the particles, that is, with the notation of the introduction, when the force in the kinetic equation is

\displaystyle\Gamma(t,x,v)=\varrho(t,x)(u(t,x)-v)-\nabla_{x}[p(\varrho)](t,x).

This additional factor also appears in the feedback of particles on the fluid, that is in the ource term in the Navier-Stokes equations. We are thus led to consider the following system:

(10.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\nabla_{x}p\cdot\nabla_{v}f+{\rm div}_{v}[f\varrho(u-v)-f\nabla_{x}p(\varrho)]&=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u)&=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}(\alpha\varrho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\alpha\varrho u\otimes u)+\alpha\nabla_{x}p-\Delta_{x}u-\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}\,u&=\varrho(j_{f}-\rho_{f}u),\\[5.69054pt]
\alpha&=1-\rho_{f}.\end{aligned}\right.

Our main result of local well-posedness is the following.

Theorem 10.1
.

Consider the same assumptions of Theorem
1.5
. Assume also that
f^{\mathrm{in}}
is compactly supported in velocity. Then the conclusion of Theorem
1.5
holds for the density-dependent drag case of System (
10.1
).

To fix notations, let us assume that

(10.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\,f^{\mathrm{in}}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathrm{B}(0,M^{\mathrm{in}}),\ \ M^{\mathrm{in}}>0.
